      Reporting

       By default, the mlcal specific machine-learning commands create a report folder in the
       node directory. The report folder contains results of created data (for create_ml and
       generate_data_ml), prediction results (for predict_ml and reverse_search_ml), and
       results for checking accuracy of the ML model (for train_ml). The reported items can be
       visualized using parallel coordinates plot from Sentaurus Calibration Workbench GUI. See
       Creating Parallel Coordinate Plots on page 167.

       In addition, for the commands which include DoE data generation functions, you can
       activate the generate_pairplot epilogue which generates a correlation pairplot image [16]
       for parameter and target data, respectively. See generate_pairplot on page 248. The image
       includes a histogram for each target, allowing you to check the approximate distribution. In
       addition, the figure checks whether there are targets showing positive/negative correlation
       through linear fitting among different targets.

      Plotting Data

       The command plot_data allows you to plot the data after an mlcal task. The supported
       plot types are curve, hist, line, parallel_coordinates, scatter, and surface, and it
       generates graphs based on two different backends, mpl (for matplotlib), and plotly. The
       mpl produces static images, while the plotly backend generates interactive graphs, which
       have characteristics of user interaction. For example,

          mlcal_obj.plot_data(targets=targets, parameters=parameters,
                                              x="PWELL", y=["Ron_at200A", "Eoff_mJ"],
                                              plot_type="scatter", backend="plotly",
                                              outfile_prefix="MLoptimization")

       For more information, see plot_data on page 260.

Generating Model Training/Test Data

There are two different ways to generate initial model data. The first is by using the
generate_data_ml task. The second is by generating the data at the same time as creating
the model using create_ml. Using create_ml is the simplest and most compact way of
doing this, but sometimes it is beneficial to explicitly separate out data generation from the
other calibration steps and use generate_data_ml instead.

There are three ways in which a DoE (design-of-experiments, or set of experimental
parameter input values) can be set in generate_data_ml:

· By setting a specific DoE and number of experiments

· By passing specific experimental parameters via a csv or DataFrame

· By taking the values from reverse search results

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  66

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       The first and second cases are described in this section. The third will be described in
       Reverse Search on page 76, because it is meant to be used as a "check" of model
       predictions rather than for initial data generation.

       For the complete set of task arguments, see generate_data_ml on page 245.

      Using generate_data_ml With a Specific DoE

       The simplest way of using generate_data_ml is to run a specific DoE within the parameter
       space defined in the passed parameters. For example, running 100 experiments
       (doe_size=100) uniformly randomly sampled (doe_method="uniform") is accomplished
       with:

          mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                           doe_method="uniform", doe_size=100,
                                                           save_report=True)

       This creates a child project in the (previously) specified directory which contains these run
       experiments. It then saves that data as a csv file from which this data can be inspected or
       loaded. An equivalent method of running this set of experiments is:

          mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                           data_generation={doe_method="uniform",
                                                           doe_size=100}, save_report=True)

       This second method is usually used when additional arguments are used to define the DoE.
       For example, if you want to run the same set of experiments but also include the current
       values of the parameters and a specific random seed of 2, you can use the following
       command:

          mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                           data_generation={doe_method="uniform",
                                                           doe_size=100, include_nominal=True,
                                                           random_seed=2}, save_report=True)

       The DoE's available in Sentaurus Calibration Workbench are "box-behnken",
       "full-factorial", "gaussian", "grid", "latin-hypercube", "uniform", "sobol", and
       "adaptive". The doe_size argument is not required for the full-factorial method
       ("full-factorial") and the Box-Behnken method ("box-behnken"). For the grid DoE, doe_size
       can be either a single integer value (wherein the grid has the same number of points for
       each parameter) or a list of integer values (wherein the grid has different numbers of points
       for each parameter). For example, to specify a grid with 3 sample points along the first
       parameter and 5 for the second parameter (15 samples in total):

          mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                           doe_method="grid", doe_size=[3,5],
                                                           save_report=True)

       The length of the list must be equal to the number of parameters.

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  67

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

To include the nominal values of parameters in the DoE also, set include_nominal=True
in the data_generation dictionary. By default, the nominal value of each parameter is the
middle point between the minimum and maximum values of the parameter
(use_mean=True). If use_mean=False, then the user-provided initial parameter value is
used as the nominal value.

force_pass Parameters and Data Generation

If a Parameter has choices set and has force_pass=False, then it is treated just like the
Parameters with minimum and maximum values by the DoE. As an example, consider
generating data using two parameters as inputs. The first has a maximum and minimum set,
while the other uses 2 discrete choices. If the data is generated with a uniformly random
DoE with doe_size=50, both the continuous parameter and the discrete parameter are
randomly selected with 50 experiments in total.

 p_time = Parameter(name="time", value=5.0, minimum=1.0,
                                    maximum=10.0)

 p_temp = Parameter(name="temperature", value="300",
                                    choices=["300", "400"], dtype=str)
                                    ...

 mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                   doe_method="uniform", doe_size=50,
                                                   save_report=True)

The resulting generated data would have 50 experiments in total and look similar to:

...        time  temperature ...

...        5.3   300  ...

...        8.7   400  ...

...        6.4   400  ...

...        3.3   300  ...

...        ...   ...  ...

...        9.8   400  ...

However, if force_pass=True, then the DoE is run for each set of choices. So, if the DoE is
uniformly random with doe_size=50, then 50 uniformly random samples are generated for
each condition set, which is 100 experiments (2 x 50):

 p_time = Parameter(name="time", value=5.0, minimum=1.0,
                                    maximum=10.0)

 p_temp = Parameter(name="temperature", value="300",
                                    choices=["300", "400"], dtype=str,
                                    force_pass=True)

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                        68

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

                                    ...
 mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,

                                                   doe_method="uniform", doe_size=50,
                                                   save_report=True)

The resulting data that is generated has 100 experiments in total and look similar to:

...        time  temperature ...

...        5.3   300  ...

...        8.7   300  ...

...        6.4   300  ...

...        3.3   300  ...

...        ...        ...

...        9.8   300  ...

...        5.3   400  ...

...        8.7   400  ...

...        6.4   400  ...

...        3.3   400  ...

...        ...   ...  ...

...        9.8   400  ...

Adaptive Data Generation

Adaptive data generation is a special DoE in that rather than all experiments being set
initially and run in parallel, the data is generated in batches of randomly generated
("uniform") experiments, with an additional machine learning model (called the "filter") used
to monitor the results and increase the number of "passed" experiments. Adaptive data
generation runs until the number of passed experiments matches that of the desired DoE
size.

An experiment is considered "passed" for a model if:

· It completes successfully without error

· All target values are within their specified range

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                                            69

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

Note:

    If using adaptive data generation with create_ml, it uses only targets that are
    contained within the model.

The machine learning model used for the filter must be a classifier type model. In this case,
it is classifying experiments as "pass" or "fail" based on their input parameters. After a batch
is run, the filter is (re-)trained using all previously generated data. When a new batch of
experiments is generated, their parameters are passed through the filter to determine
whether they pass or fail. Those experiments that are likely to pass are kept, while those that
fail are replaced with new experiments. This continues until all the sets of experimental
parameters for that batch are likely to pass. After that, the batch of experiments runs, and
additional data is generated. See Figure 2 for a simplified flow diagram.

Figure 2   Simplified flow of adaptive data generation method

If you want to generate 500 passed experiments (doe_size=500) by running batches of 50
experiments (batch_size=50) and using a decision tree classifier to use previously run
experiments to predict pass/fail experiments (classifier=DecisionTree), the function call
is:

 mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                   data_generation={
                                                   "doe_method": "adaptive",

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                        70

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

                                   "doe_size": 500,
                                   "batch_size": 50,
                                   "classifier": DecisionTree},
                                   save_report=True)

In many cases, you might want to specify an initial DoE to run before switching to random
generation (initial_sampling_doe), the number of batches to use that initial DoE for
(initial_doe_batches), a certain number of generated samples that must "pass" before
using the ML filter, and a transformation to apply to the model inputs (transformer). The
function call then looks like:

 mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                   data_generation={
                                                   "doe_method": "adaptive",
                                                   "doe_size": 500, "batch_size": 50,
                                                   "threshold_num_pass": 100,
                                                   "initial_sampling_doe": "sobol",
                                                   "initial_doe_batches": 2,
                                                   "transformer": Standard,
                                                   "classifier": DecisionTree},
                                                   save_report=True)

The supported transformers and classifiers are given in Table 9 and Table 10.

Table 9    Supported transformers

Keyword    Transformer

Identity   No transformer applied

Standard   Mean-standard scaler

MinMax     Min-max scaler

Quantile   Quantile transformer

Robust     Robust scaler

Table 10 Supported classifiers

Keyword    Classifier                         Hyperparameter Ref.

AdaBoost   AdaBoost classifier                [17]

DecisionTree Decision tree classifier         [18]

GPC        Gaussian process classifier [19]

KNNC       K Nearest neighbors classifier [20]

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                                   71

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

Table 10 Supported classifiers

Keyword          Classifier                   Hyperparameter Ref.

MLPC             Multilayer perception classifier [21]

Each transformer or classifier has different hyperparameters. The hyperparameters and
their default values follow the rules of the respective scikit-learn methods. The
hyperparameters can be set in the data_generation dictionary:

 data_generation={"doe_method": "adaptive", ...,
                                "scaler_hyperparameters": {...},
                                "classifier_hyperparameters": {...}, ...}

For example, you can select the neural network classifier ("classifier": MLPC), which
has hyperparameters such as hidden_layer_sizes and activation, each with default
values [2]. You can change the values of some hyperparameters by specifying:

 data_generation={"doe_method": "adaptive", ...,
                                "classifier": MLPC, ...,
                                "classifier_hyperparameters":
                                {"hidden_layer_sizes": (32, 16, 4),
                                "activation": "tanh",
                                "learning_rate": "adaptive"}, ...}

Using generate_data_ml by Passing Experimental Parameters

Data can also be generated by passing the specific parameter values via csv or pandas
DataFrame. This is done by setting the doe argument. The columns of the csv/DataFrame
should be the parameter names and the rows should be the values of the parameters for
each experiment. So, for a function call of:

 p0 = Parameter(name="Param0", value=0.0, minimum=-5.0, maximum=5.0)
 p1 = Parameter(name="Param1", value=1.0, minimum=-5.0, maximum=5.0)
 parameters = [p0, p1]
 mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,

                                                   doe="test_exp.csv", save_report=True)

the structure of "test_exp.csv" should be:

...        Param0 ...           Param1 ...

...        2.0   ...            2.5           ...

...        1.5   ...            0.3           ...

...        -3.0  ...            4.5           ...

...        ...   ...            ...           ...

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                                           72

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       Running generate_data_ml then creates a child project and runs experiments with these
       specific input parameters and stores them in the child project directory. Note that other
       columns can be present in the csv/DataFrame without an issue.

      Data Generation Using create_ml

       The task create_ml can either load data from previously generated experiments or it can
       generate the initial data itself. It contains the doe_method, doe_size, and
       data_generation arguments, which work the same way as those arguments in
       generate_data_ml. A typical create_ml function call to create an ML model and also run
       a latin hypercube DoE with 100 experiments might be:

          mlcal_obj.create_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                              targets=targets, doe_method="latin-hypercube",
                                              doe_size=100)

       Note:
            This does not train the machine learning model with the generated data. More
            about this is discussed in later sections.

      Re-Running Data Generation

       When running an mlcal project, experiments are re-run in the child Sentaurus Workbench
       project only if a successfully completed one with those input parameters does not exist. That
       is, an experiment is run only if:

       · The experiment does not already exist in the child Sentaurus Workbench project

       · The experiment exists in the child Sentaurus Workbench project, but it failed

       Mlcal does not automatically delete these child projects after the Sentaurus Calibration
       Workbench project has completed. If cleanup of the child project is desired, this must be
       performed manually.

      Creating the Machine Learning Model

       While the Model class is used to define the machine learning models used, create_ml is the
       task that creates the (untrained) Sentaurus Calibration Workbench machine learning object.
       This is saved as {ml name}_untrained.ml in the project directory. In Sentaurus Calibration
       Workbench, this object also contains the training/test data associated with it and so this data
       is required as an input. If the experimental data is provided in "exp_data.csv", the function
       call is:

          mlcal_obj.create_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                              targets=targets, train_data="exp_data.csv")

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  73

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       Alternatively, as described in Data Generation Using create_ml on page 73, create_ml can
       also generate the data to be used for training the model. For the complete set of task
       arguments, see create_ml on page 233.

Training the Machine Learning Model

After the model has been created, it needs to be trained. This is performed by train_ml. A
simple function call for training the models for a given set of parameters and targets is:

mlcal_obj.train_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters, targets=targets

The trained model is saved as {ml name}_trained.ml in the project directory. When
training, the data is randomly split into two sets: train data and test data. 90% of the model
data set becomes the train data set, which is used for training the model. The remaining
10% is the test data set used to assess the quality of the model predictions on a set of data
that it has yet to see. The train_ml task also outputs the performance of the model on both
these sets, including plots comparing the predicted values to the actual values.

Normally, if a trained model already exists, it is not be re-trained. To re-train an existing
trained model (rather than just loading it), the retrain argument must be set to True:

mlcal_obj.train_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                  targets=targets, retrain=True)

This retrain is different from the retrain model hyperparameter. A summary of how these
work in together is given in Table 11.

Table 11   Model and train_ml retrain combinations

train_ml "retrain"  Model "retain"  Model           Effect if trained model exists
argument            Hyperparameter  "warm_start"
                                    Hyperparameter

True                ---             ---             Untrained model loaded and training
                                                    performed.

False               ---             ---             Existing trained model loaded

False               True            True            Existing trained model loaded and

                                                    warm_start applied.

For the complete set of task arguments, see train_ml on page 299.

Data Trimming

The data used for training can also be trimmed. The criteria for this are the same as for
adaptive data generation, in which the target values must all be within specified ranges. For
trimming to work, the target being used as a trimmer must be included in the model. To filter

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                                             74

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       the training set, set the data_set argument to "trimmed", "passed", or "passedtrimmed".
       The target range defined in create_ml becomes "passed" and the target range defined in
       train_ml becomes "trimmed". To select only trimmed data:

          mlcal_obj.train_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                            targets=targets, data_set="trimmed")

       To have the full data set used, set data_set to "full":

          mlcal_obj.train_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                            targets=targets, data_set="full")

       Even with the "full" data set, failed experiments and experiments for which the profile
       target file does not exist are automatically trimmed.

Using the Machine Learning Model

Once you have a trained machine learning model, you can use it for either the prediction of
targets, given specific inputs (predict_ml) or for finding parameters that results in specific
target values (reverse_search_ml).

predict_ml

predict_ml works very similarly to generate_data_ml in that it takes input parameters and
outputs a csv file of results. To have the models predict the value of a list of targets based
on specific input parameters using a uniformly random DoE with 100 experiments, the
predict_ml function call is:

 mlcal_obj.predict_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                        targets=targets, doe_method="uniform",
                                        doe_size=100)

In addition to using specific DoEs, the predict_ml task can be run on the data used to train
and test the model. There are three options for the doe_method that specify this data:
"model_data", "model_train_data", "model_validate_data" (which is the test data
set). If you want to see the model predictions for the test data set, the function call is:

 mlcal_obj.predict_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                        targets=targets,
                                        doe_method="model_validate_data")

Like generate_data_ml, predict_ml can also take as an input argument a csv file or
pandas DataFrame that defines specific parameter values to be run:

 mlcal_obj.predict_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                        targets=targets, doe="test_exp.csv")

For information about the required csv table format, see Using generate_data_ml by
Passing Experimental Parameters on page 72.

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  75

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       Normally, making predictions outside of the parameter bounds of the model (extrapolation)
       is not allowed and results in an error. However, if you set check_boundary=False, then a
       only warning is issued:

          mlcal_obj.predict_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                targets=targets, doe="test_exp.csv",
                                                check_boundary=False)

       For the complete set of task arguments, see predict_ml on page 264.

      Reverse Search

       Reverse search (using a model for finding parameters that result in specific target values) is
       relatively straightforward but has some idiosyncrasies in how it should and can be used
       compared to the other functions. It is not possible to work backward from the target values
       to the specific parameters, so instead the parameter space must be explored using the ML
       model(s) and the parameters values that result in the closest target values selected. A basic
       reverse search that uses a uniform random DoE and selects the top 10 candidates is:

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="uniform", doe_size=100000)

       Note that doe_size should be set quite large because the machine learning models can run
       very quickly when compared to TCAD simulations. In the this function call, the candidate
       values are the best ones found using the DoE. To perform a further gradient-based
       optimization on these candidates, the optimize argument should be set to True:

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="uniform", doe_size=100000,
                                                             optimize=True)

       In addition to using a DoE, specific values to search can be set using the doe argument in
       the same manner as is used for predict_ml or generate_data_ml. The parameter ranges
       can also be specified using a DataFrame or csv file, using the range argument.

       For the complete set of task arguments, see reverse_search_ml on page 271.

       Adaptive Reverse Search

       Unlike the other reverse searches, adaptive reverse_search is performed in multiple steps.
       At each step, a uniform random DoE is run with a size of doe_size. From the results of this
       search, the best candidates and parameter search space are updated. This continues for
       num_steps steps, or until the time limit given by time_limit has been reached.

       When performing an adaptive reverse search, some of the arguments are given as kwargs.
       If For example, if you define an adaptive reverse search task with a doe_size at each step
       of 1000, a time limit of 1 hour, and a maximum number of steps of 100, the function call is:

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  76

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="adaptive", doe_size=1000,
                                                             **{"num_steps": 100,
                                                             "time_limit": 3600})

       Some key adaptive reverse search kwargs are:

       · num_steps (int): Specifies the number of searching steps

       · time_limit (int): Specifies the time limit

       · individual_rms (bool): If True, include individual rmse in addition to the total rmse

       · search_method (str): Specifies the search method: "min_tol" or "min_rms". "min_tol"
            prioritizes all targets within their tolerances, while "min_rms" considers only the rmse.

       Priority targets are given extra weight when assessing the result of an adaptive reverse
       search. They are specified using the individual target IDs:

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="adaptive", doe_size=1000,
                                                             priority_targets=["t_Y0","t_Y1"],
                                                             **{"num_steps": 100,
                                                             "time_limit": 3600})

       For more information, see reverse_search_ml on page 271.

       Multi-Modal and Multi-Objective Reverse Search

       Unlike the conventional DoE methods, the NSGAIII-based DoE methods, NSGAIII_S and
       NSGAIII_M, perform in multiple steps. NSGAIII_S is a DoE method designed for
       multi-modal optimization. Using NSGAIII_S increases the likelihood of identifying multiple
       minima points in the total RMS error space. On the other hand, NSGAIII_M is used for
       multi-objective optimization. When there are multiple targets, NSGAIII_M provides
       candidates by considering not only the total RMS error but also the individual error of each
       target. These two methods determine the population size and number of generations based
       on the given DoE size. For more detailed adjustments, additional arguments can be defined
       in dictionary form like in the following example:

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="NSGAIII_S", doe_size=10000,
                                                             args_nsgaIII={"pop_size": 100,
                                                             "n_points": 100, "n_max_gen":1000,
                                                             "mutation_prob": 0.1})

       For more information, see reverse_search_ml on page 271.

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  77

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       Clustering Method for Candidate Selection

       reverse_search_ml has a clustering feature. With the clustering option enabled, it forms
       clusters of candidates and extracts the candidates with the lowest total error from each
       cluster. This increases the likelihood of finding diverse candidate combinations across a
       broader parameter space. The clustering option includes both a fixed number and optimal
       number of clusters. With the fixed number option, a specified number of clusters is
       generated. With the optimal number option, it automatically determines the optimal number
       of clusters. It generates image files (pair plot and parallel coordinates plot) to visualize the
       clusters. (The label "0" is the selected best candidates and the others are the cluster
       numbers.)

       Chains of reverse_search_ml

       Unlike other tasks, the outputs from reverse_search_ml are stored in the mlcal object and
       added to with each subsequent run. So, if you run two reverse searches in a row, such as:

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="uniform", doe_size=100000)

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="sobol", doe_size=100000)

       The first reverse search saves a csv with 10 candidates and the second reverse search
       stores a csv with 20 candidates: the results from both reverse_search_ml calls. This
       allows you to build up a series of reverse search results.

       To reset the reverse search results prior to performing the search, the reset_results
       argument should be set to True. So, if you modify the second reverse_search_ml with this
       argument:

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="uniform", doe_size=100000)

          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="sobol", doe_size=100000,
                                                             reset_results=True)

       Each of the reverse searches now return 10 candidates, the results from each search.

       Updating Parameter Values and Ranges

       After performing a reverse search, the parameter space can be updated based on the
       results of the search. This is set using the update_parameters argument. If it is set to
       value, then the parameter values are set to those of the best candidate found using reverse
       search. If it is set to range, then in addition to the parameter values being set, the parameter
       ranges are to the maximum and minimum values in the set of candidates.

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  78

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       An example use is chaining together a series of reverse searches, narrowing the reverse
       search space, and then searching again and evaluating the result:

          #Performs an initial set of reverse searches and narrows the range
          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,

                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=50,
                                                             doe_method="uniform", doe_size=100000)
          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=50,
                                                             doe_method="sobol", doe_size=100000,
                                                             update_parameters="range")

          #Resets the results and searches in the narrowed range
          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,

                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             optimize=True, doe_method="uniform",
                                                             doe_size=100000,
                                                             update_parameters="value",
                                                             reset_results=True)

          #Runs an evaluation using the best reverse search candidate
          mlcal_obj.evaluate(parameters=parameters, targets=targets)

       Checking With generate_data_ml

       When generate_data_ml is run after a reverse search and the DoE is left unspecified, it
       uses the candidates found using reverse_search_ml as the input experiments. This is
       usually the preferred approach to using evaluate, because even well-trained models can
       have a certain degree of error in their predictions and it makes sense to evaluate multiple
       candidates. In such a case, the script of the previous section becomes:

          #Performs an initial set of reverse searches and narrows the range
          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,

                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=50,
                                                             doe_method="uniform", doe_size=100000)
          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=50,
                                                             doe_method="sobol", doe_size=100000,
                                                             update_parameters="range")

          #Resets the results and searches in the narrowed range
          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,

                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="uniform", doe_size=100000,
                                                             update_parameters="value",
                                                             reset_results=True)
          mlcal_obj.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,
                                                             targets=targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                             doe_method="sobol", doe_size=100000,
                                                             update_parameters="value")

          #Evaluates the 20 candidate parameter sets and selects the best one

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  79

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

          mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                           child_dir="@pwd@/../results_child",
                                                           update_parameters=True)

       Note that a new child directory is specified in the generate_data_ml step. This is because
       an additional identifying parameter is added to reverse search results and are therefore not
       compatible with the previous child Sentaurus Workbench projects.

      Additional Topics

      Validating Parameter Ranges

       In many cases, the parameter ranges may have been set without running experiments other
       than the nominal case. This can mean that when the TCAD simulations are run at the
       extrema of the parameters, there may be many failures and/or errors. The function
       validate_range_ml checks and adjusts the parameter ranges so that the child
       experiments pass for the extreme values of each parameter. It is called with:

          mlcal_obj.validate_range_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets)

       This adjustment is performed by starting with the nominal parameter values, and then
       setting each individual parameter to their maximum and minimum values, with all other
       parameters remaining at the nominal values. If an experiment fails at a particular minimum/
       maximum parameter value, this value is increased (minimum) or decreased (maximum) and
       then re-tested. This continues until every experiment passes or a maximum number of
       iterations. The increase/decrease ratio and maximum number of iterations can be set with
       the reducing ratio and num_validations arguments:

          mlcal_obj.validate_range_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                             reducing_ratio=0.1, num_validations=7)

       You can also add additional constraints that must be met via the constraint argument. For
       example, if an output variable of Fail must be equal to 0 to be considered a valid
       experiment, then you call:

          mlcal_obj.validate_range_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                             reducing_ratio=0.1, num_validations=7,
                                                             constraint="Fail==0")

       For the complete set of task arguments, see validate_range_ml on page 301.

      Sensitivity Analysis and Primary Parameters

       In many cases, there are a substantial amount of input parameters, but not all may have a
       significant effect on the target values. It can therefore be valuable to assess how sensitive
       the target output response is to the different input parameters and keep only the subset of

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  80

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       parameters that have a significant impact. In mlcal, this is accomplished by performing a
       sensitivity analysis.

       A local sensitivity analysis can be run using the following command:

          mlcal_obj.run_sensitivity_analysis_ml(parameters=parameters,
                                                                               targets=targets)

       The result can be found in the report folder for the node. For cases where simulations should
       be retried upon a failure or results should be averaged from multiple simulations, a
       num_retries argument and a num_evaluations argument are available. If you want to
       have retry upon failure and used sensitivity results averaged from 5 experiments, the
       function call is:

          mlcal_obj.run_sensitivity_analysis_ml(parameters=parameters,
                                                                               targets=targets,
                                                                               num_retries=2,
                                                                               num_evaluations=5)

       Note:
            The DoE used to determine sensitivity in run_sensitivity_analysis_ml is
            different from that used in scwcal's run_sensitivity_analysis.

       To get a list of the primary parameters based on a previously run sensitivity analysis, the
       determine_primary_parameters_ml function should be used:

          param_list =

                              mlcal_obj.determine_primary_parameters(parameters=parameters)

       This parameter list can then be used in any following step. By default, the number of primary
       parameters returned is 8. If a different number is desired, this can be set using the
       num_parameters argument. A minimum sensitivity value can also be set using the
       min_sensitivity argument. For example, if only the top 5 parameters than have a
       sensitivity greater than 0.05 are needed, then the function call is:

          param_list =
          mlcal_obj.determine_primary_parameters(parameters=parameters,

                                                                                 num_parameters=5,
                                                                                 min_sensitivity=0.05)

       This particular function call returns a list of up to 5 parameters. If less than 5 parameters
       meet the min_sensitivity constraint, then the parameter list includes only those that meet
       the minimum sensitivity constraint. By setting the number of parameters high, every
       parameter that meets the minimum sensitivity constraint can be returned.

       For more information about each function, seerun_sensitivity_analysis_ml on page 276 and
       determine_primary_parameters_ml on page 238.

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  81

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

Merge Tables

Sometimes, a single experiment (or row of data) outputs (or contains) multiple data points
that should all be captured in a single parameter and target. For example, when varying the
RF power as an input parameter for etching, each experiment might output etch depths at
several different times:

           power time_0 depth_0 time_1 depth_1 time_2 depth_2

Exp 1 100 1.0        2.5  2.0                 4.5  3.0  5.5

Exp 2 200 1.0        3.5  2.0                 6.5  3.0  8.5

...        ...  ...  ...  ...                 ...  ...  ...

In Sentaurus Calibration Workbench, these are normally set as individual parameters and
targets:

 p_power = Parameter(name="power", value=150.0, minimum=0.0,
                                      maximum=300.0)

#time parameters are extracted from the data, not set
p_time0 = Parameter(name="time_0", minimum=0.0, maximum=5.0,

                                    is_extracted=True)
p_time1 = Parameter(name="time_1", minimum=0.0, maximum=5.0,

                                    is_extracted=True)
p_time2 = Parameter(name="time_2", minimum=0.0, maximum=5.0,

                                    is_extracted=True)

 t_depth0 = Target(name="depth_0", value=3.0, dimension=0)
 t_depth1 = Target(name="depth_1", value=5.0, dimension=0)
 t_depth2 = Target(name="depth_2", value=7.0, dimension=0)

However, for the ML model, you may want just a "time" parameter and "depth" target that
encompasses all the individual times and depth. This is done through a merge table. First,
in addition to the above, you must declare the additional time and depth parameters:

 #time parameters are extracted from the data, not set
 p_time = Parameter(name="time", minimum=0.0, maximum=5.0,

                                    is_extracted=True)
 t_depth = Target(name="depth", value=3.0, dimension=0)

Next, you must declare a merge table that specifies which parameters and targets to merge
and the names of the merged parameter and target:

 merge_table = {"time":["time_0", "time_1", "time_2"],
                             "depth":["depth_0", "depth_1", "depth_2"]}

Note that the length of the lists for each merged parameter and target must match, because
they will be paired with each other for training (that is, the depth given by "depth_0" is at
"time_0"). When creating the ML object, all of the parameters and targets should be passed

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                        82

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

       to it. However, for subsequent steps using the ML models, only the merged values should
       be used:

          create_params = [p_power, p_time0, p_time1, p_time2, p_time]
          create_targets = [t_depth0, t_depth1, t_depth2, t_depth]
          ml_params = [p_power, p_time]
          ml_targets = [t_depth]

          ml_obj.create_ml(models=models, parameters=create_params,
                                         targets=create_targets,
                                         train_data=["etch_data.csv"],
                                         merge_table=merge_table)

          ml_obj.train_ml(models=models, parameters=ml_params,
                                       targets=ml_targets)

          ml_obj.predict_ml(models=models, parameters=ml_params,
                                           targets=ml_targets, doe_method="sobol",
                                           doe_size=100)

          ml_obj.reverse_search(models=models, parameters=ml_params,
                                                  targets=ml_targets, num_candidates=10,
                                                  doe_method="sobol", doe_size=100000,
                                                  update_parameters="value")

       Merge Tables and Reverse Search
       One of the challenges with using merge tables is that after performing a reverse search, it is
       difficult to perform a check using generate_data_ml or evaluate. This is because the
       found parameters and targets are the merged ones and are not necessarily inputs and
       outputs of the parent project.

       Because of this, it is recommended to have a setup that can take the merged parameters as
       input and report the merged output targets. For the previous example then, you may want a
       parent project that uses "time" as an input that defines the complete etch time and "depth"
       as the final etch depth. So, you might generate data with:

          p_power = Parameter(name="power", value=150.0, minimum=0.0,
                                              maximum=300.0)

          #time parameters are extracted from the data, not set
          p_time0 = Parameter(name="time_0", minimum=0.0, maximum=5.0,

                                              is_extracted=True)
          p_time1 = Parameter(name="time_1", minimum=0.0, maximum=5.0,

                                              is_extracted=True)
          p_time2 = Parameter(name="time_2", minimum=0.0, maximum=5.0,

                                              is_extracted=True)

          t_depth0 = Target(name="depth_0", value=3.0, dimension=0)
          t_depth1 = Target(name="depth_1", value=5.0, dimension=0)
          t_depth2 = Target(name="depth_2", value=7.0, dimension=0)

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  83

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

#By setting is_extracted=True, the parameter match the parent
p_time = Parameter(name="time", minimum=0.0, maximum=5.0,

                                  is_extracted=True)
t_depth = Target(name="depth", value=3.0, dimension=0)

create_params = [p_power, p_time0, p_time1, p_time2, p_time]
create_targets = [t_depth0, t_depth1, t_depth2, t_depth]

 ml_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=create_params,
                                             targets=create_targets, doe_method="sobol",
                                             doe_size=500)

Assuming that the "time" parameter of the parent project is set to 3.0 and it corresponds to
the total etch time and also "time_2", the data in the saved csv should look similar to that in
the following table.

           power time time_0 depth_0 time_1 depth_1 time_2 depth_2 depth

Exp 1 100 3.0 1.0    2.5                      2.0  4.5  3.0         5.5  5.5

Exp 2 200 3.0 1.0    3.5                      2.0  6.5  3.0         8.5  8.5

...        ...  ...  ...                      ...  ...  ...         ...  ...

Now, you may run the same model creation and training as in the previous merge table
example:

 ml_params = [p_power, p_time]
 ml_targets = [t_depth]

ml_obj.create_ml(models=models, parameters=create_params,
                               targets=create_targets,
                               train_data="etch_data.csv",
                               merge_table=merge_table)

ml_obj.train_ml(models=models, parameters=ml_params,
                             targets=ml_targets)

 ml_obj.reverse_search(models=models, parameters=ml_params,
                                         targets=ml_targets, num_candidates=10,
                                         doe_method="sobol", doe_size=100000,
                                         update_parameters="range")

Even though the "time" and "depth" parameter are in the data table, they are ignored in favor
of the merged parameter and target. To run generate_data_ml, you must first set the "time"
parameter to no longer be extracted and then run.

 p_time.is_extracted=False

mlcal_obj.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,
                                                 child_dir="@pwd@/../results_child",

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                                          84

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

                                                           update_parameters=True)

Examples and Use Cases

Basic Workflow

The following snippet shows a very basic workflow with a simple set of steps to optimize two
parameters to match targets at two different conditions. This is meant to serve as an
example of the simplest possible mlcal calibration algorithm.

 from scw.core.parameter import Parameter
 from scw.core.target import Target
 from scw.apps.mlcal import MLCal
 from scw.core.model import Model

 # Defining parameters and targets
 p0 = Parameter(name="stickingA", value=0.05, minimum=0.01,

                             maximum=0.2)
 p1 = Parameter(name="yield", value=1.0, minimum=0.05, maximum=2.0)
 c1 = Parameter(name="time", value="10", choices=["10","20"],

                             force_pass=True, dtype=str)

 t_yA = Target(name="depth", value=1.6776,
                           conditions={"time": "20"})

 t_yB = Target(name="depth", value=0.9386,
                           conditions={"time": "10"})

 parameters = [p0, p1, c1]
 targets = [t_yA, t_yB]

 # Declare project options
 mlcal0 = MLCal()
 mlcal0.set_option(parent="@pwd@/../parent/example_parent")
 mlcal0.set_option(child_dir="@pwd@/../child")
 mlcal0.set_option(tools=["stopo_step1"])
 mlcal0.set_option(queue="lsf:all")

 # Evaluate
 mlcal0.evaluate(parameters=parameters, targets=targets)

 # Define Machine-Learning Model
 hyperparameters = dict()
 hyperparameters["depth"] = {"model_type": MLPR,

                                                   "model_hyperparameters":
                                                   {"hidden_layer_sizes": [8, 8],
                                                   "solver": "lbfgs"},
                                                   "retrain": True,
                                                   "parameter_scaler": Standard}
 ml_0 = Model(name="ml_0", hyperparameters=hyperparameters)
 models = [ml_0]

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                                        85

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

          # Generate TCAD Data in child project
          mlcal0.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,

                                                      doe_method="sobol", doe_size=128)

          # Create machine learning model using generated data
          mlcal0.create_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,

                                         targets=targets, train_data=
          ["@pwd@/results/nodes/1/n1_report/2_ML_generate/2_ML_generate.csv"])

          # Train the machine learning model
          mlcal0.train_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,

                                       targets=targets,
                                       retrain=True)

          # Perform a reverse search using the machine learning model
          # parameters that result in the target values
          mlcal0.reverse_search_ml(models=models, parameters=parameters,

                                                       targets=targets, doe_method="sobol",
                                                       doe_size=100000, num_candidates=10)

          # Generate TCAD data for the 10 candidate parameter sets
          mlcal0.generate_data_ml(parameters=parameters, targets=targets,

                                                      child_dir="@pwd@/../results_child",
                                                      update_parameters=True)

          # Report best result from the TCAD data
          mlcal0.print_parameters(parameters=parameters)
          mlcal0.print_targets(targets=targets)
          for target in targets:

                 print(f"{target.name} Error:
                                {mlcal0.get_rms_error()}")

      Search for High Peak-Gain HBT Device Geometries

       This example studies the DC current-voltage behavior of a heterojunction bipolar transistor
       (HBT) with respect to the device geometries. One simulation flow consists of three steps:
       The input parameters include device geometries (width and thickness, shown as arrows in
       Figure 3), and the doping levels at the emitter and base. There are 15 parameters in total
       (13 geometric parameters and 2 doping parameters). The device can be of either npn type
       or pnp type. In this example, the type takes npn as a constant.

       Three outputs are extracted from the simulation. The DC Gummel plot includes the change
       of the base current, the collector current with respect to the base-emitter voltage, and the
       ratio between the collector current and the base current as the gain (see Figure 4). The

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  86

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

voltage and gain at the peak of the gain-voltage curve is extracted as PeakGain and
Vb_PeakGain.

Tool name (tool label) Description            Command file          Preference file
                                              sde_dvs.cmd           sde_dvs.prf
Sentaurus Structure  Generates device
Editor (sde)         structure

Sentaurus Device     Stimulates device        Gummel_des.cmd        Gummel_des.prf
(Gummel)

Sentaurus Visual     Generates Gummel         PlotGummel_vis.cmd PlotGummel_vis.prf
(PlotGummel)         plot and extracts
                     measurements

The collector current at Vb_PeakGain is extracted as Ic_PeakGain. The three extracted
values are returned in the Sentaurus Visual step using the puts "DOE: $name $value"
mechanism.

Usually, the PeakGain is of the order of magnitude of hundreds. The question this example
solves is whether it is possible to have an HBT device structure with a peak gain above
1000, while Ic_PeakGain is low (below 1e-5 A/m).

Figure 3   Device structure with geometric parameters labeled

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                                         87

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
Machine Learning Calibration Module: mlcal

Figure 4   Gummel plot showing extracted targets in example

Sentaurus Calibration Workbench MLCal Script

 # Import libraries
 from scw.core.scwglobal import *
 from scw.core.parameter import Parameter
 from scw.core.target import Target
 from scw.apps.mlcal import MLCal
 from scw.core.model import Model

 # Definition of parameters and targets
 g0 = Parameter("G0", 0.075, minimum=0.0375, maximum=0.1125)
 g1 = Parameter("G1", 0.225, minimum=0.1125, maximum=0.3375)
 g2 = Parameter("G2", 0.45, minimum=0.225, maximum=0.675)
 g3 = Parameter("G3", 0.1, minimum=0.05, maximum=0.2)
 wcol = Parameter("Wcol", 0.2, minimum=0.1, maximum=0.3)
 wemi = Parameter("Wemi", 0.15, minimum=0.075, maximum=0.225)
 dwc = Parameter("dWc", 0.1, minimum=0.05, maximum=0.15)
 dwb = Parameter("dWb", 0.075, minimum=0.0375, maximum=0.1125)
 xeb = Parameter("Xeb", 0, minimum=0, maximum=0.32)
 xbc = Parameter("Xbc", 0.16, minimum=0, maximum=0.32)
 hsub = Parameter("Hsub", 0.3, minimum=0.15, maximum=0.45)

      Fitting for Etching Profile From Data File

       This example demonstrates how to load data from a file, apply name-merging rules and
       encoding methods for a high-dimensional profile, and perform training, prediction, and
       reverse search. The data is prepared before the machine-learning practice and has been
       extracted into data.csv files.

       The features include process parameters (gas flow, C4F8 gas ratio, power, ...) and the times
       at which different profiles are taken (time_01, time_02, and time_03). The outs are three
       profile files for each of those time steps: profile_01, profile_02, and profile_03. The profile
       files are CSV files containing the Z and AverageRadius columns, which define the profile.

       During the training, the three time steps and profiles are merged, so that the
       machine-learning model can predict the resulting profile with the given experiment
       conditions and time.

High-Level Machine Learning Functions

       The high-level machine learning functions include all the elements of a machine-learning
       based calibration or optimization algorithm as described earlier in this chapter. However, the
       machine learning models used by these algorithms are predefined and need not be set by
       you. Whereas the functions of the previous section are unique to mlcal, the following
       functions are included in all modules. They consist of:

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  92

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
High-Level Machine Learning Functions

       · explore_ml: Explores the parameter space using a DoE and then finding the best
            candidates using reverse search

       · refine_ml: Performs a more local search and DoE than explore_ml but also trains a
            machine learning model and uses reverse search to find the best candidates

       · calibrate_ml: Uses both explore_ml and refine_ml as part of a general calibration
            strategy

      explore_ml and refine_ml

       Both explore_ml and refine_ml use very similar high-level algorithms. They both have
       relatively large function signatures:

          mlcal_obj.explore_ml(parameters=<t>, targets=<t>, [parent=<s>,]
                                                [child_dir=<s>,] [task_name=<s>,]
                                                [tolerance=<f>,] [num_iterations=<i>,]
                                                [num_parameters=<i>,]
                                                [explore_doe_range=<f|i>,]
                                                [explore_doe_method=<k>,]
                                                [explore_doe_size=<i>,] [explore_doe=<s|p>,]
                                                [explore_doe_sep=<s>,] [sensitivity=<b|s|d>,]
                                                [constraint=<s>,] [priority_targets=<t>,]
                                                [num_retries=<i>,] [num_evaluations=<i>])

          mlcal_obj.refine_ml(parameters=<t>, targets=<t>, [parent=<s>,]
                                                [child_dir=<s>,] [task_name=<s>,]
                                                [tolerance=<f>,] [num_iterations=<i>,]
                                                [num_parameters=<i>,] [sensitivity=<b|s|d>,]
                                                [constraint=<s>,] [num_retries=<i>,]
                                                [num_evaluations=<i>])

       To assist with understanding all of the arguments, a flowchart that summarizes the algorithm
       used in both functions is shown in Figure 5. The details of each step can differ between
       refine_ml and explore_ml. After every step, all of the experiments previously run are
       assessed to determine which result is the best and whether the tolerance has been met. If the
       tolerance is met, then a final evaluation is performed.

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  93

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
High-Level Machine Learning Functions

Figure 5   Flowchart description of explore_ml and refine_ml tasks

                          Error < `tolerance'                 RMSE and Noise                           `sensitivity'=True OR
                                                                                             0 < `num_parameters' < total params
                                                              (`num_evaluations' used)
                                                                                                  Sensitivity Analysis
                                                                               else
                                                                                             (keep`num_parameters' params)

   FinalCheck             Error < `tolerance'                       Generate Data                                                Error < `tolerance'

(`num_evaluations' used)  Error < `tolerance'                                          else   Adjust Search Space

                          Error < `tolerance'                 Create and Train Models        else
                                Error < `tolerance'OR
                                Iterations  `num_iterations'                           else

                                                                  Reverse Searches

                                                                                      else

                                                               Run/ Check Candidates

RMSE and Noise: The parent project is evaluated num_evaluations times. The RMSE is
taken from the average and the variation between the simulated values is assessed to see
whether a calibration within tolerance is likely possible. This is mostly a concern for TCAD
simulations where there can be run-to-run variation.

Sensitivity Analysis: If only a subset of parameters are to be used during calibration, then
a sensitivity analysis is run to determine which parameters have the greatest impact on the
target values. Based on this analysis, the top num_parameters parameters are used during
the following calibration/optimization.

Generate Data: Child Sentaurus Workbench projects are run and data generated. This
experiment differs between explore_ml and refine_ml. In explore_ml, the experiment is
defined by the user, while refine_ml uses a DoE predefined in Sentaurus Calibration
Workbench. The key arguments used to define the DoE used by explore_ml are:

· explore_doe_range (float or filename): Sets the parameter range scaling used for
    the DoE. A value of 1.0 means the full range. If a file name is provided, then it sets the
    name of a CSV file that specifies the parameter ranges for the DoE.

· explore_doe_method (keyword): Sets the DoE method. Options are "box-behnken",
    "full-factorial", "gaussian", "latin-hypercube", "uniform", "sobol", and
    "custom".

· explore_doe_size (int): Sets the number of samples for the DoE.

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide                                                                                                          94

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
High-Level Machine Learning Functions

       · explore_doe (filename or DataFrame): Sets the DoE of the CSV file path or pandas
            data frame when explore_doe_method is "custom".

       Create and Train Models: Machine learning models are created and trained on the
       generated data.

       Reverse Searches: A series of reverse searches are run using the trained models to try and
       find the best set of parameters to match the targets. Several candidate parameter sets are
       suggested. The accuracy of priority_targets is given extra weight during the reverse
       searches.

       Run/Check Candidates: Sentaurus Workbench experiments are run using the candidate
       parameter sets and the best selected. If the result is within tolerance or the maximum
       number of iterations has been exceeded, then the algorithm moves to a final check of the
       best results.

       Adjust Search Space: If the result is not within tolerance and the maximum number of
       iterations has not been reached, the search space is adjusted based on the results so far.
       This differs between explore_ml and refine_ml, with explore_ml changing the
       parameter bounds while keeping a relatively broad search space and refine_ml focusing
       more on moving the center point of the parameter space. Higher num_iterations tend to
       be more useful for refine_ml than for explore_ml. A single iteration for explore_ml and
       then moving on to refine_ml or another method is often the most efficient course of action.

       Final Check: The Sentaurus Workbench project is evaluated using the best parameter set
       it found num_evaluations times. The RMSE is taken from the average.

       For more information, see explore_ml on page 243 and refine_ml on page 270.

      calibrate_ml

       The calibrate_ml function iterates through the explore_ml and refine_ml steps to find
       parameters that result in the desired target values. Like explore_ml and refine_ml, it has
       many possible arguments:

          calibrate_ml(parameters=<t>, targets=<t>, [parent=<s>,]
                                  [child_dir=<s>,] [task_name=<s>,] [tolerance=<f>,]
                                  [num_iterations=<i>,] [num_validations=<i>,]
                                  [num_parameters=<i>,] [num_explore_parameters=<i>,]
                                  [num_refine_parameters=<i>,] [explore_doe_range=<f>,]
                                  [explore_doe_method=<k>,] [explore_doe_size=<i>,]
                                  [tolerance_refine=<f>,] [strategy=<s>,]
                                  [validate_range=<b>,] [sensitivity=<b|s|d>,]
                                  [constraint=<s>,] [priority_targets=<t>,]
                                  [num_retries=<i>,] [num_evaluations=<i>])

SentaurusTM Calibration Workbench User Guide  95

W-2024.09
Chapter 3: Sentaurus Calibration Workbench Machine-Learning Module
References

       Either explore_ml or refine_ml is called until the RMSE error is less than tolerance or
       num_iterations has been reached. The decision to run refine_ml or explore_ml or
       validate_range is based on several factors.

       In Table 12, the parameters which affect each step within the calibrate_ml task are
       shown. They are equivalent to the same setting for the individual function(s). In addition,
       there are arguments that adjust the flow of the calibrate_ml algorithm:

       · tolerance (float): Sets the total relative RMS error tolerance

       · tolerance_refine (float): Sets the tolerance at which all subsequent iterations must
           be refine_ml. Note that refine_ml may be called even if this tolerance is not yet met as it
           only defines one case in which it would be called.

       · validate_range (bool): Specifies whether to perform an initial parameter range
           validation analysis.

       · strategy (string): Specifies the strategy. For good initial parameter values and
           ranges, specify "standard". "standard" strategy performs the range validation and the
           sensitivity analysis at the beginning of the workflow. If the initial parameters are far from
           optimal or the nominal simulation failed, then specify "validate". The "validate" strategy
           performs several times of the range validations and the sensitivity analysis during while
           running the calibration.
